Hello everyone. I wanted to make this video and properly explain. Most importantly though, their values align with Quompi and what it's always stood for doing things properly and helping young researchers enter the quantitative space. This transition is going to allow Quonai to continue to be a learning resource without being limited to me. what this means. So, the channel is going to stay up. I want to say thank you to everyone who's watched the video, left a comment or reached out. Quai started during my masters in financial mathematics as a way for me to tutor online and it grew into something much bigger than that because of this community. I'm genuinely grateful for that. So treat that responsibility with the respect it deserves. This isn't goodbye, it's a transition. ## 28. Stochastic Volatility Models used in Quantitative Finance
**Channel:** QuantPy | **Views:** 35K | **Date:** 3 years ago | **Duration:** 7:40 | **ID:** afaGdQcAEaI
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=afaGdQcAEaI

### Transcript:
in this video we're going to be talking about stochastic volatility models in quantitative finance here we'll be talking about the people that have contributed the who what why and when and we're going to be investigating some aspects of the models that are most important it all started in 1973 where investors for many decades would be thanking the work that went on in this year this of course is the option pricing model that was proposed by black and shoals that actually had a closed form solution of course the assumption here is constant volatility in 1976 the first stochastic volatility models were actually produced by merton and cox and ross merton actually assumed a mixture of continuous and jump processes while cox and ross actually allowed for return volatility to be a deterministic function of underlying stock price in 1976 the leverage effect was actually found by black this is where we actually compare the returns and the volatility distributions and black worked out that there's a negatively correlated relationship between returns and volatility although not really used for evaluation in 1982 the arch model was developed by engel to actually account for the non-constant volatility non-constant volatility is also called heteroskedasticity and that's why the model was called auto-regressive conditional heteroscedasticity and this really tried to model the realized variances over time engel received the nobel prize for his work now an extension to the arch model was actually developed by engel's phd student boler's sleeve now below sleeve tried to think about how price changes not only depend on past price changes but also on past estimated volatility this extension to the arch model involve using moving averages in 1987 the first stochastic volatility model was made by helen white they analyzed european call options on a stock price subject to stochastic volatility and using taylor series expansion they derived an accurate formula for call options this was semi-closed with no correlation between returns of volatility their assumption was relaxed in 1988 now on monday the 19th of october 1987 the dow jones dropped more than 20 percent within one day and this would later be called black monday and since then the prevalence of volatility smiles and smirks has been evident in option markets and hence why black shoals model in the use is very controversial in 1991 another stochastic volatility model was created by stan and stein these two worked on a unique method of using a mean reverting process called the einstein ulenbek process to actually model variants their original works assume no correlation between returns and volatilities now in 1993 there was a very important concept and this was the introduction of the vix index at the time they were only using the s p 100 and eight strikes on puts and calls to determine the index as well in 1993 a stochastic volatility model was created by heston this incorporated modeling a square root process of variants the hester model is a popular choice because of its easy closed form solution that does require numerical integration it has no negative variances and it incorporates the leverage effect first identified by black in 1994 a local volatility model was proposed by de pere now this is where market prices were used to actually determine this local volatility surface in terms of time and strike this deterministic surface could be used to value other options incredibly at the same time derman and carney were working on a similar model this perfectly fits observed volatility surface in 1996 this man on the left-hand side came up with a new way of thinking about stochastic volatility he actually created a jump diffusion and stochastic volatility model bates used the best aspects of the heston's 1993 model in merton's jump model in 1994 and then incorporated this in-between stochastic volatility with jump diffusion this led to a new area of research in 1998 rough volatility models were created by compti and renault this is where the use of fractional brownian motion was used to actually measure long-term persistent memory in markets in 2002 realized variants was investigated by bond off nielsen and shepard they created a new way to actually estimate real world variances this was incredible because of the incorporation of high frequency data in 2003 a new way and methodology of actually creating the vix index came about this was an update from the previous use of s p 100 options and now incorporated s p 500 as the weighted prices of different puts and calls in 2004 the introduction of vix futures by cboe was based on the fix index and suddenly allowed for an increased amount of risk management and volatility trading in 2006 cboe introduced vix options and this also increased the pool of which trading strategies could be developed for risk management purposes and volatility trading 2008 was very historic in terms of the vix index whereby the index reached its highest intraday point at 89.53 on october the 24th [Music] in 2009 the double heston model by christopherson heston and jacobs was developed this is where model in two variances processes and one actually turns the correlation between returns and volatility stochastic this was a major advancement in stochastic volatility models now although we've covered a lot in this video from 1973 all the way to 2009 there are many more models including the cev and sabah models the 3-2 and the 4-2 models local stochastic volatility models stochastic volatility models with jumps exponential levy models and svi parameterization etc the current benchmarks seem to be modelers that are actually based on variance curves and skew modeling now creating a video that incorporated all of the stochastic volatility models would have been a fool's errand but hopefully this actually served to just introduce the concept of stochastic volatility models since the start of option valuation in 1973 with a black shoals model now if you enjoyed the video please give it a thumbs up and check out my channel where we actually go through all things quantitative finance

in this lesson you are going to learn how to reduce the variance of your monte carlo simulation like a quant now what do i mean by that well essentially whenever you want to use monte carlo as a method for valuing derivatives and options essentially what you're doing is you're simulating all these different paths from these underlying distributions and you're taking samples now the more samples that you take usually the better estimate you're going to get but instead of doing that because it's computationally intensive to then reduce the error and to reduce that standard error that deviation that you have around your estimate what we're going to do is we're going to look for something that's very highly correlated with the option product that we're dealing with in this case we're going to look at delta based control variants now we're going to exploit this function the fact that delta is a perfect correlation with the option price to be able to actually incorporate that ## 43. Market Makers || Payment for Order Flow Explained || Market Maker and Exchange Incentives
**Channel:** QuantPy | **Views:** 11K | **Date:** 4 years ago | **Duration:** 23:06 | **ID:** LtniMCE9uTA

### Transcript:
payment for order flow is something that you may have heard of because one of the most popular retail brokerage firms of late robin hood actually declared that it doesn't have brokerage fees so if they don't if a brokerage company doesn't have brokerage fees how are they making money where is their revenue coming from and of course the answer is payment for order flow so the questions we're going to be tackling here is what is payment for order flow how much can you make from it is there a difference between security types what are the major firms that benefit and is there any disbenefit to the retail trader so let's get into that in the video so let's explain what payment for order flow actually is it's a simple transaction between a market-making firm and a brokerage firm now why would they pay well essentially it's a payment to actually get orders get the orders of retail traders to that particular market maker now why would a market maker want order flow what benefit is there in getting volume of transactions well the market maker is trying to make the difference between the bid and the ask price so a market maker is anyone that's willing to be bid at a certain price and also be on the other side and have an asking price on the screen now the difference between these two prices in the marketplace the price that you can buy and the price that you can sell is called the bid ask spread now the more liquid a particular asset is the smaller that bit r spread is so for the example here we have a lot of the stocks on the s p 500 have very small spreads now in comparison some of the derivative markets so the options on those very underlines have larger spreads now regulators do have strict rules on how far this spread can be given the timelines of some derivatives for example here in the um on the asx you can see here that there's been a fundamental change on different timelines just recently since october the first this year that market makers have to have liquidity so this is really essential to understand that there are limits in this bid-r spread now how can a market maker make money from this bid-r spread well essentially as they fill orders they're trying to capture as much of that bid-r spread as possible so they love retail traders because they're often transacting in smaller volumes and therefore are happy to carry the risk on certain directions it's very easy for them to capture as much of that bid ask spread as possible so if they're benefiting by netting off the risk between the buys and sells that retail investors make throughout the day then the aim of the game in making that bid r spread is a volume so how do you get that volume well one way here is obviously paying brokers where all the retail investors are trying to submit orders to paying the brokers money for that order flow for the flow of orders from those retail traders and getting their executions in executing their price the market maker is taking on quite a lot of risk depending on the product they're taking on directional risk in whichever way it comes and it's constantly changing so for that risk they are being compensated the bid ask spread and really it comes down to the fee structure and the arrangements that these exchanges have with market makers so it's important to understand that a market maker has special arrangements with exchanges to actually execute these orders in the end now for example the new york stock exchange has a maker taker framework so this economic model is where if you add to the liquidity so non-marketable orders that aren't going to be executed but add to the liquidity of that order book then you're paid for that service if on the other hand you transact and it's a marketable order then you're taking away liquidity from the order book so you have to pay a fee for that so the difference between that fee structure the payments and fees from the market exchange is how the market exchange makes money so obviously what the market maker is trying to do is limit the amount that it actually pays the exchange and they're trying to maximize the amount of volume that they have um on that bid ask spread and therefore earning as much money as possible now who gets that order flow and who doesn't so now that we've established that market makers like increased volume because the more volume that they have the more executions that they have and then overall that's leading to a larger volume net profit in terms of that bid-r spread so what does that mean how do they actually get the order flow well essentially on a lot of the brokers websites they have to state a number of times that all market makers are paid equally so what i mean by that is that for a certain order type for a certain order size each broker no matter who they are are going to be paid the same amount so how come you can see in some of the brokers that more money funnels to different market making firms well that's the competitive advantage that some market making firms have for example citadel is one of the largest market makers in terms of some of the us retail brokerage firms and you can see that in the chart here this is done on a bit of a bidding process internally so the bid ask spread that you can actually see on an exchange is really the upper limit if however certain market makers have a lot of order flow they can start price competing against other market makers so really the person with the most amount of volume the most amount of visibility in the marketplace is really the winner because they can reduce that bid ask spread and earn smaller margins but then transact more often so really these firms are quite incentivized to get you the best price possible because then they win more volume so you'll often see on websites of brokerage firms like robin hood here that there is some kind of net improvement in transactions so this is compared to if you were just transacting with something that you've actually put a price to i want to execute at 23.50 so once you've listed that price if you're buying for example then if that 23.50 is below the ask price in the marketplace then where you transact in the bank account and the underlying to hedge some of the directional risk that you have while holding your options now for anyone who intimately knows the option pricing methodology and formulation this portfolio replication is actually the basis of how to price options so how can we go about actually hedging this risk well essentially we're going to enter into an the underlying from making those adjustments those adjustments accumulated up are going to make the difference of actually hedging some of that directional risk so if you're long an options position
